- this equivalence may be obtained by applying
[
HKK24
, Lem. 2.1.16]
to the adjunction
\infl^{e}_{G}\colon\mathcal{S}\rightleftharpoons\mathcal{S}_{G}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu(-)^{G}
.

The wrong–way map
Eq.

5
satisfies the following key vanishing result permitting our characterisation of
C_{p}
–Poincaré spaces. By virtue of the lemma, the cofibre of
Eq.

5
may be viewed as measuring the “geometric free part” of the dualising sheaf
D_{\underline{{X}}}
.

Lemma 3.3.2
.

Let
\underline{X}\in\mathcal{S}_{C_{p}}^{\omega}
and let
\nu\colon\mathrm{Sp}_{C_{p}}\rightarrow eanexactfunctorwhichvanisheson
Sp
^
ind
_C_p
.Thenthemap$$\nu(\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{X}})\longrightarrow\nu(\varepsilon^{*}\varepsilon_{!}D_{\underline{X^{C_{p}}}})$$in
Fun
(X^C_p,induced by
Eq.

5
is an equivalence.

Proof.

We have to show, for any
x\in X^{C_{p}}
, that the map

\nu(D_{\underline{{X}}}(x))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\nu(f_{!}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}(x))
is an equivalence.
First, let us show that for any compact
C_{p}
-space
\underline{{X}}
the map
\varepsilon\colon\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}\rightarrow\underline{{X}}
induces an equivalence

\nu(X_{*}(-))\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}\nu(X^{C_{p}}_{*}\varepsilon^{*}(-)).

If
\varepsilon\colon\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}\rightarrow\underline{{X}}
is an equivalence, this is a tautology. The class of spaces for which the assertion is true is moreover stable under pushouts, retracts and contains
\underline{{Y}}=\underline{{C_{p}/e}}
, as

0\simeq\nu(\emptyset_{*}\epsilon^{*}(-))\simeq\nu(\underline{{C_{p}/e}}_{*}(-))\simeq\nu(\underline{{C_{p}/e}}_{!}(-))\simeq\nu(\mathrm{Ind}_{e}^{C_{p}}(-))\simeq 0.

Using that
X_{*}\simeq X_{!}(D_{\underline{{X}}}\otimes-)
and
X^{C_{p}}_{*}\epsilon^{*}\simeq X^{C_{p}}_{!}(D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}\otimes\epsilon^{*}-)\simeq X_{!}(\epsilon_{!}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}\otimes-)
we obtain the equivalence

\overline{\nu}(X_{!}(D_{\underline{{X}}}\otimes\--))\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}\overline{\nu}(X_{!}(\varepsilon_{!}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}\otimes\--)).

Now consider a fixed point
x\colon*\rightarrow X^{C_{p}}
(which we also view as
x\colon\underline{\ast}\rightarrow\underline{X}
).
Note that the projection formula provides an equivalence, natural in
E\in\mathchoice{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.46193pt\raisebox{0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.46193pt\raisebox{-0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.35355pt\raisebox{-0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.19133pt\raisebox{-0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{-0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{-0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{-0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\mbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}}}{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{-0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{-0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.46193pt\raisebox{-0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.35355pt\raisebox{-0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.19133pt\raisebox{-0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{-0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{-0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{-0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}}}^{\underline{X}}

X_{!}(E\otimes x_{!}(\mathbb{S}_{C_{p}}))\simeq X_{!}x_{!}(x^{*}E)\simeq x^{*}E=E(x).

Thus, for any
x\in X^{C_{p}}
, the map

\nu(D_{\underline{{X}}}(x))\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}\nu(\epsilon_{!}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}(x))
is an equivalence, whence the result.
∎

We are now ready to prove our main result characterising
C_{p}
–Poincaré spaces. Note that, unlike
Lemma

3.3.2
, the key characterising property is given solely in terms of
D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}
and does not involve
D_{\underline{{X}}}
.

Theorem 3.3.3
.

Let
\underline{{X}}
be a compact
C_{p}
-space for which
X^{e}
and
X^{C_{p}}
are (nonequivariant) Poincaré spaces. Then
\underline{{X}}
is
C_{p}
-Poincaré if and only if the cofiber

\cofib(D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}\rightarrow\varepsilon^{*}\varepsilon_{!}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}})^{e}\in\func(X^{C_{p}},\mathrm{Sp}^{BC_{p}})

pointwise lies in the stable subcategory
\mathrm{Sp}^{BC_{p}}_{\mathrm{ind}}\subseteq\mathrm{Sp}^{BC_{p}}
.

Proof.

As
X^{e}
is assumed to be Poincaré the specta
D_{\underline{{X}}}(y)=D_{X^{e}}(y)\in\mathrm{Sp}
are invertible for all
y\in X^{e}
.
Furthermore, as
X^{C_{p}}
is Poincaré we know that
\Phi^{C_{p}}D_{\underline{{X}}}(x)=D_{X^{C_{p}}}(x)
is invertible for all
x\in X^{C_{p}}
.
It now follows from
Lemma

3.0.1
that
\underline{{X}}
is
C_{p}
-Poincaré if and only if the
C_{p}
-spectrum
D_{\underline{{X}}}(x)
is compact for all points
x\in X^{C_{p}}
.

Note that all maps in the bottom right cospan in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\underline{{X}}}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{!}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(6)

induce equivalences on geometric fixed points: the map
f
by
Lemma

3.3.2
, and the map
g
by
[
HKK24
, Lem. 4.2.3]
. We can use
Lemma

3.1.5
to complete
Eq.

6
to a commutative square of
C_{p}
-spectra where
F
is compact and all maps are equivalences on geometric fixed points.
Consider the exact functor

\nu=\left(\mathrm{Sp}_{C_{p}}\xrightarrow{(-)^{e}}\mathrm{Sp}^{BC_{p}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}^{BC_{p}}/\mathrm{Sp}^{BC_{p}}_{\mathrm{ind}}\right).

Note that as
g^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}
are maps between compact
C_{p}
-spectra that induce an equivalence on geometric fixed points,
Lemma

3.2.3
shows that
\nu(\cofib(f^{\prime}))\simeq\nu(\cofib(g^{\prime}))\simeq 0
, so
\nu(f^{\prime})
and
\nu(g^{\prime})
are equivalences.

Let us first assume that
\underline{{X}}
is
C_{p}
-Poincaré.
It follows from
Lemma

3.3.2
that
\nu(\cofib(f))\simeq 0
, so also
\nu(f)
is an equivalence.
Thus,
\nu(g)
is an equivalence from which we obtain
\nu(\cofib(g))\simeq 0
, proving one direction of the claim.

For the other direction, assume
\nu(\cofib(g))\simeq 0
, i.e. that
\nu(g)
is an equivalence.
As before, as
\nu(f)
and
\nu(f^{\prime})
are equivalences we obtain that
\nu(\cofib(g^{\prime}))\simeq 0
.
By definition of
\nu
, this means
\cofib(g^{\prime})^{e}\in\mathrm{Sp}^{BC_{p}}_{\mathrm{ind}}
.
Now,
F\in\mathrm{Sp}_{C_{p}}
and
D_{\underline{X}}(x)\in\mathrm{Sp}_{C_{p}}
both have compact underlying spectra. Hence, it follows from
Lemma

3.2.3
that
\cofib(g^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Sp}_{C_{p}}
is compact.
But then
D_{\underline{X}}(x)
is compact too, as was to be shown.
∎

4
Genuine virtual Poincaré duality groups

In this section, we define a refinement of the classical notion of
virtual Poincaré duality groups
. Recall that a Poincaré duality group is a discrete group
\pi
such that
B\pi
is a Poincaré space, and a group is a virtual Poincaré duality group if it contains a Poincaré duality group of finite index. In this case, every finite index torsionfree subgroup will be a Poincaré duality group.

Now, if
\Gamma
is a discrete group and
\pi
a finite index torsionfree normal subgroup, then the space
B\pi
can be enhanced to a
\Gamma/\pi
-space in a canonical way by viewing it as the quotient

of the universal space for the family of finite subgroups of
\Gamma
.
One might naturally wonder if that
\Gamma/\pi
-space is
\Gamma/\pi
-Poincaré, in which case we call
\Gamma
a
genuine virtual Poincaré duality group
.
In fact, we will give a slightly more general definition that also includes the case where
\Gamma
is a Lie group.
Heuristically, genuine virtual Poincaré duality groups are those which capture the homotopical properties of groups for which the universal space of proper actions admits a smooth manifold model.

4.1
Universal spaces for proper actions

Let us first collect some examples and constructions for universal spaces for proper actions from the literature.

Definition 4.1.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a Lie group. By
\mathrm{Cpt}

we denote the
family of compact subgroups of
\Gamma
.
The
universal space for proper actions
is the universal space for the family
\mathrm{Cpt}
, and is denoted by
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
.

If
\Gamma
is discrete, the family of compact subgroups of
\Gamma
agrees with the family of finite subgroups, and we denote it by
\mathrm{Fin}
.

Example 4.1.2

(
[
Abe78
]
, Thm 4.15.)
.

Assume the Lie group

acts properly, smoothly and isometrically on a simply connected complete Riemannian manifold

\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}

\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
.

Example 4.1.3

(
[

.

Suppose,

Then a barycentric subdivision of the

of

\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}

\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}

More examples for geometrically interesting models for the universal spaces for proper actions can be found in the survey

]
and the references therein.
For the next statement, recall that for a subgroup

, its
normaliser
is defined as
N_{\Gamma}H\coloneqq\{g\in\Gamma\mid gHg^{-1}=H\}
and its
Weyl group
as
W_{\Gamma}H\coloneqq N_{\Gamma}H/H
.

Remark 4.1.5
.

To prove
Theorem

4.1.4
one checks that
Eq.

□

is a pushout on all fixed points by distinguishing the three cases
H=1
,
H\neq 1
finite and
H
infinite.
Examples of groups for which it applies are discrete groups
\Gamma
for which there exists
\pi
torsionfree and an extension

1\rightarrow\pi\rightarrow\Gamma\rightarrow C_{p}\rightarrow 1.

\Gamma

Example 4.1.6
.

Let us give an illustrative geometric example.
Consider the group
\Gamma=p3
, the symmetry group of the wallpaper depicted in
Footnote

2
, with its action on the euclidean plane.
This action is isometric and has finite stabilisers, so by
Example

4.1.2
it is a model for the universal space of proper action of
p3
.

Note that the translations form a normal torsionfree subgroup of
p3
of index 3.
We can apply
Theorem

4.1.4
to obtain that the subspace of the plane with nontrivial
\Gamma
-isotropy is
\Gamma
-homotopy equivalent to

.
Now from the picture it is easy to read off that the singular part is indeed discrete and consists of three
\Gamma
-orbits.
We conclude that
\Gamma
has precisely three conjugacy classes of nontrivial finite subgroups, and each such nontrivial finite
F\subset\Gamma
satisfies
N_{\Gamma}F=F
.

Three points with
C_{3}
-symmetry, corresponding to three conjugacy classes of finite subgroups of
p3
.

4.2
Equivariant Poincaré duality for groups

For the following, a closed subgroup
\pi\subset\Gamma
of a Lie group is
cocompact
if the topological space
\pi\backslash\Gamma
is compact. If
\pi\subset\Gamma
is normal, this is equivalent to
\Gamma/\pi
being a compact Lie group.

Definition 4.2.1
.

\Gamma
be a Lie group and let
\pi\subset\Gamma
be a cocompact torsionfree discrete normal subgroup. We write

for the quotient of
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}

by the action of

.

The following definition is supposed to capture the homological properties of Lie groups
\Gamma
that admit a cocompact smooth manifold model for
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Cpt}
(and a discrete torsionfree normal cocompact subgroup).
If
\Gamma
is torsionfree, it reduces to
\Gamma
being a Poincaré duality group. Here, and only here we refer to Poincaré duality for compact Lie groups as also developed in
[
HKK24
]
, but the reader mainly interested in discrete group actions can assume
\Gamma
to be discrete throughout.

Definition 4.2.2
.

Let
\Gamma
be a Lie group. Then
\Gamma
is called a
genuine virtual Poincaré duality group
if
it has a cocompact torsionfree normal subgroup, and if for any such cocompact torsionfree normal subgroup
\pi\subset\Gamma
, the
\Gamma/\pi
-space

\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
is a
\Gamma/\pi
-Poincaré space.

is
\Gamma/\pi^{\prime}
-Poincaré.
∎

Note that, in general, the intersection of two cocompact subgroups is not again cocompact, e.g. for
\mathbb{Z},\sqrt{2}\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}
.
In the case where
\Gamma
is discrete, the intersection of two finite index subgroups is again finite, from which we obtain the following result.

Corollary 4.2.4
.

Suppose,
\Gamma
is a discrete group. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

The group
\Gamma
is a genuine virtual Poincaré duality group.

(2)

There exists some torsionfree finite index normal subgroup
\pi\subset\Gamma
such that the
\Gamma/\pi
-space
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
\textstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi
is
\Gamma/\pi
-Poincaré.

5
Extensions by
C_{p}

In this section, we study genuine virtual Poincaré duality groups sitting in an extension

1\to\pi\to\Gamma\to C_{p}\to 1

(7)

more closely.
In
Section

5.1
we will prove the characterisation from
Theorem

C
;
in
Section

5.2
we will use this to prove property (H) for pseudofree extensions.

5.1
Characterisation of genuine virtual Poincaré duality groups

Lemma 5.1.1
.

Consider an extension of groups of the form
Eq.

7
where
\pi
is torsionfree. Write
\mathcal{M}
for a complete set of representatives of the conjugacy classes of nontrivial finite subgroups of
\Gamma
.

(1)

If
F\leq\Gamma
is a nontrivial subgroup with
\pi\cap F=e
(e.g.
F
is finite), then the composition of
F\rightarrowtail\Gamma\to C_{p}
is an isomorphism. In particular,
F
is a maximal finite subgroup of
\Gamma
.

B_{\Gamma}\pi$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi$}}}}^{C_{p}}\simeq\coprod_{F\in\mathcal{M}}BW_{\Gamma}F.

Proof.

Point (1) follows as the kernel of
\Gamma\rightarrow C_{p}
is torsionfree, so every finite subgroup of
\Gamma
will inject into
C_{p}
. For (2), observe that if
\pi
acts freely on a
\Gamma
-space
\underline{{Y}}
, then the map

(\pi\backslash\underline{{Y}}^{>1})^{C_{p}}\rightarrow(\pi\backslash\underline{{Y}})^{C_{p}}

is an equivalence by
Fact

2.1.8
.
Thus, since applying
(\pi\backslash-)^{C_{p}}
to the top row in the pushout square
Eq.

□

yields the map of empty spaces, we get an identification

Also, the composition
\pi\cap N_{\Gamma}F\subset N_{\Gamma}F\rightarrow W_{\Gamma}F
is an isomorphism. Indeed, it is injective, as it has at most finite kernel and
\pi
is torsionfree, and it is surjective as
N_{\Gamma}F
is generated by
F
and
\pi\cap N_{\Gamma}F
, and
F
maps to zero in
W_{\Gamma}F
.

(8)

of

(10)

for
F
a subgroup with
\pi\backslash(\Gamma/F)\simeq*
(so that
F
is nontrivial) and
\pi\cap F=e
.
From
Lemma

5.1.1
we now learn that the map
F\subset\Gamma\rightarrow C_{p}
is an isomorphism. Hence,
F
can be used to define a section
s\colon C_{p}\rightarrow\Gamma
.
We will now construct the following diagram.

Restricting the pullback
Eq.

9
along
s
, we obtain the outer cartesian square of
C_{p}
-spaces in

is an equivalence, which is checked easiest by looking at the map on underlying spaces and
C_{p}
-fixed points.
The square labeled
(B)
is obtained by restricting the pullback
Eq.

9
along the section

. By virtue of

being a section of
\Gamma\rightarrow C_{p}
, and as inflation is nothing but restriction along a surjective group homomorphism, we have
\res^{C_{p}}_{\Gamma}\infl^{C_{p}}_{\Gamma}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{S}_{C_{p}}}
, explaining the identifications in square
(C)
. It now follows from
Lemma

5.1.3
below that the upper left corner has exactly one fixed point, as required.

B_{\Gamma}\pi$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi$}}}}^{C_{p}}

is the point

by

10
.
∎

Lemma 5.1.3
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group for which each nontrivial finite subgroup is contained in a unique maximal finite subgroup.
Then for maximal finite subgroups
F,F^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
we have that
F
acts freely on
\Gamma/N_{\Gamma}F^{\prime}
if
F
and
F^{\prime}
are not conjugate and
(\Gamma/N_{\Gamma}F^{\prime})^{F}=*
if
F
and
F^{\prime}
are conjugate.

Proof.

Suppose there is
f\in F\backslash e
and
g\in\Gamma
such that
fgN_{\Gamma}F^{\prime}=gN_{\Gamma}F^{\prime}
.
Then
g^{-1}fg\in N_{\Gamma}F^{\prime}
so the subgroup generated by
F^{\prime}
and
g^{-1}fg
is finite.
By maximality of
F^{\prime}
, we obtain
g^{-1}fg\in F^{\prime}
.
The nontrivial element
f
lies in both maximal finite subgroups
F
and
gF^{\prime}g^{-1}
of
\Gamma
which agree by uniqueness.
This shows that the
F
-action on
\Gamma/N_{\Gamma}F^{\prime}
is free if
F
and
F^{\prime}
are not conjugate.

In the other case it suffices to show that
(\Gamma/N_{\Gamma}F)^{F}=*
.
One fixed point is clearly given by
eN_{\Gamma}F
.
Suppose that there are
f\in F\backslash e
and
g\in\Gamma
such that
fgN_{\Gamma}F=gN_{\Gamma}F
.
The argument from above shows that
F=gFg^{-1}
, i.e.
g\in N_{\Gamma}F
.
∎

We now come to the proof of our main characterisation result for genuine virtual Poincaré duality groups coming from
C_{p}
–extensions.

Theorem 5.1.4
.

Consider an extension of groups

1\rightarrow\pi\rightarrow\Gamma\rightarrow C_{p}\rightarrow 1

where
\pi
is a Poincaré duality group, and assume that the
\Gamma
-space
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
\textstyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}
is compact. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

The group
\Gamma
is a genuine virtual Poincaré duality group.

(2)

For each nontrivial finite subgroup
F\subset\Gamma
, the Weyl group
W_{\Gamma}F
is a Poincaré duality group.

Proof.

\pi\backslash(-)

B_{\Gamma}\pi$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{-0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{-0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi$}}}}^{C_{p}}\simeq\coprod_{F\in\mathcal{M}}BW_{\Gamma}F

(11)

where
F
runs through a set
\mathcal{M}
of representatives of the conjugacy classes of nontrivial finite subgroups. If a space is Poincaré, then each individual component is Poincaré. So we learn that
W_{\Gamma}F
is a Poincaré duality group for each
F\in\mathcal{M}
. As conjugate subgroups have isomorphic Weyl groups, this implies that the conclusion holds for each nontrivial finite subgroup
F
.

B_{\Gamma}\pi$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle B_{\Gamma}\pi$}}}}
in the following.
From
Lemma

5.1.2
, we get a cartesian square of
C_{p}
-spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

(12)

where
\underline{{T}}=\underline{{*}}\coprod\underline{{S}}
and
\underline{{S}}
is a disjoint union of free
C_{p}
-orbits where, furthermore, the point
x
corresponds to the image of the composite
as
, where
s\colon\underline{{*}}\to\underline{{T}}
denotes the section coming from the fixed point of
\underline{{T}}
.

Now observe that the map
D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}(x)\to j^{*}j_{!}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}(x)
identifies with the map
s^{*}a^{*}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}\to s^{*}p^{*}p_{!}a^{*}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\underline{{T}}}a^{*}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}
induced by the unit
\mathrm{id}\to p^{*}p_{!}
.
The decomposition
\underline{{T}}\simeq\underline{{S}}\coprod\underline{{*}}
now provides a splitting

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\underline{{T}}}a^{*}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}\simeq s^{*}a^{*}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}\oplus\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\underline{{S}}}a^{*}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}|_{\underline{{S}}}

and the induced map

s^{*}a^{*}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}\to s^{*}a^{*}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}\oplus\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\underline{{S}}}a^{*}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}|_{\underline{{S}}}

(13)

is an equivalence on the first component by functoriality of colimits.
The
C_{p}
-spectrum
\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\underline{{S}}}a^{*}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}|_{\underline{{S}}}
is induced as
\underline{{S}}
is free.
Together this shows that the cofiber of the map
s^{*}a^{*}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}\to s^{*}a^{*}j^{*}j_{!}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}
lies in the subcategory
\mathrm{Sp}^{BC_{p}}_{\mathrm{ind}}\subset\mathrm{Sp}^{BC_{p}}
as was to be shown.
∎

Remark 5.1.5
.

Instead of explicitly identifying the map
\epsilon\colon D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}(x)\to j^{*}j_{!}D_{\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}}(x)
in the last step of the argument above, one can also finish the proof using the following trick. Using the splitting
Eq.

13
, one can reduce to showing that the cofiber of a selfmap
f
of the invertible spectrum
D_{\underline{{X}}^{C_{p}}}(x)
is induced. As
j
is an equivalence on
C_{p}
-fixed points, one sees that
\Phi^{C_{p}}(\epsilon)
is an equivalence. This implies that the selfmap
f
in question also is an equivalence on geometric fixed points. The Burnside congruences show that
\cofib(f)^{e}
is
n
-torsion for
n
congruent to
1
mod
p
, in particular
n
coprime to
p
. But every compact spectrum with
C_{p}
-action which is
n
-torsion for
n
coprime to
p
vanishes in
\mathrm{stmod}(C_{p})
, so it is induced.

5.2
Condition (H)

In this section we will prove an abstract version of Condition (H) for general
C_{p}
-Poincaré spaces with discrete fixed points and see how this implies Condition (H) from
Condition

1.3
.
Essential for this is the theory of singular parts and equivariant fundamental classes, especially the gluing class, introduced in
[
HKK24
, §4.5]
.
Let us recall the relevant notions and constructions here.

Construction 5.2.1

(
[
HKK24
, Cons. 4.5.4]
)
.

and collapse map
c\colon\mathbb{S}_{G}\rightarrow X_{!}D_{\underline{X}}
.
The
gluing class
of
\underline{{X}}
is defined to be the composite

\mathbb{S}\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{can}}\mathbb{S}_{G}^{hG}\xlongrightarrow{c^{hG}}(X_{!}D_{\underline{X}})^{hG}\longrightarrow\Sigma(X^{>1}_{!}\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{X}})_{hG},

where the last map is the blue composite from
Eq.

14
.
The
linearised gluing class
is obtained by postcomposing with the map induced by
\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{Z}

\Sigma(X^{>1}_{!}\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{X}})_{hG}\rightarrow\Sigma(X^{>1}_{!}\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{X}}\otimes\mathbb{Z})_{hG}.

We now specialise to the case
G=C_{p}
. Recall from
Construction

2.1.5
that for
\underline{{X}}\in\mathcal{S}_{C_{p}}
, we have
\underline{{X}}^{>1}\simeq\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}
coming from the fact that
X^{>1}\simeq X^{C_{p}}
.

Proposition 5.2.3

(Abstract condition (H))
.

Let
\underline{{X}}
be a
C_{p}
-Poincaré space with discrete fixed points such that each component of
X^{e}
has positive dimension.
Then the linearised gluing class

\mathbb{S}\rightarrow\Sigma(X^{>1}_{!}\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{{X}}}\otimes\mathbb{Z})_{hC_{p}}\xleftarrow{\simeq}\bigoplus_{y\in X^{C_{p}}}\Sigma(D_{\underline{{X}}}(y)\otimes\mathbb{Z})_{hC_{p}}

maps a generator of
\pi_{0}(\mathbb{S})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
to a generator of
\pi_{0}\Sigma(D_{\underline{{X}}}(y)\otimes\mathbb{Z})_{hC_{p}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/p
in each summand.

Recollections 5.2.4

(Invertible
C_{p}
-spectra and group (co)homology)
.

For the proof of
Proposition

5.2.3
, recall the following facts about the homology of invertible
C_{p}
-spectra.
Recall that for an abelian group with
G
-action
M
, writing
M[d]
for the corresponding object in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{BG}
concentrated in degree
d
, we have

\pi_{*}M[d]_{hG}\simeq H_{*-d}(G;M),\hskip 5.69054pt\pi_{*}M[d]^{hG}\simeq H^{d-*}(G;M),\hskip 5.69054pt\pi_{*}M[d]^{tG}\simeq\widehat{H}^{d-*}(G;M).

For
E\in\mathcal{P}\mathrm{ic}(\mathrm{Sp}_{C_{p}})
there are integers
d^{e}
and
d^{f}
such that
E^{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z}\simeq\mathbb{Z}[d^{e}]
after forgetting the
C_{p}
-action and
\Phi^{C_{p}}E\otimes\mathbb{Z}\simeq\mathbb{Z}[d^{f}]
. We write
\mathbb{Z}
for the trivial
C_{p}
-representation and
\mathbb{Z}^{\sigma}
for the sign
C_{2}
-representation.
From
[
Kra20
, Sec. 8.1.]
and elementary group homology computations we obtain
Table

1
.
In each case, if
d^{e}+1\leq d^{f}
, another group homological computation together with
Table

1
shows that the map

\pi_{d^{f}}(E^{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z})^{tC_{p}}\rightarrow\pi_{d^{f}-1}(E^{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z})_{hC_{p}}

is an isomorphism between cyclic groups of order
p
.

d^{e}-d^{f}
even

d^{e}-d^{f}
odd

p
odd

\pi_{*}E^{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z}\simeq\mathbb{Z}[d^{e}]

impossible

\pi_{d^{f}}(E^{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z})^{tC_{p}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/p

p=2

\pi_{*}E^{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z}\simeq\mathbb{Z}[d^{e}]

\pi_{*}E^{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z}\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{\sigma}[d^{e}]

\pi_{d^{f}}(E^{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z})^{tC_{p}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/p

\pi_{d^{f}}(E^{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z})^{tC_{p}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/p

Homological information on invertible
C_{p}
-spectra

to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip from
[
HKK24
, Construction 2.2.31]
preserves parametrised colimits, and the horizontal maps are induced by the adjunction counit
c\colon f_{!}f^{*}\to\mathrm{id}
.
This follows immediately from
[
Hil24
, Lemma 2.2.6]
, again using that
\Phi
preserves parametrised colimits. Importantly, the top map (and hence also the bottom map) is an equivalence by
[
HKK24
, Lem. 4.2.3]
.

, the right vertical map in

15
is compatible with this splitting.

Proof of
Proposition

5.2.3
.

By construction, the gluing class factors through the map

(X^{>1}_{!}\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{X}}\otimes\mathbb{Z})^{tC_{p}}\rightarrow\Sigma(X^{>1}_{!}\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{X}}\otimes\mathbb{Z})_{hC_{p}}

which happens to be an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
using
Recollections

5.2.4
and that
\underline{{X}}^{>1}
is a discrete
C_{p}
-space (so that
d^{f}=0
) with trivial
C_{p}
-action.
It thus suffices to show that the gluing class maps to a generator in each summand of

\pi_{0}(X^{>1}_{!}\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{X}}\otimes\mathbb{Z})^{tC_{p}}\simeq\bigoplus_{y\in X^{C_{p}}}\pi_{0}(D_{\underline{{X}}}(y)\otimes\mathbb{Z})^{tC_{p}}\simeq\bigoplus_{X^{C_{p}}}\mathbb{Z}/p.

We have the following commutative diagram.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{>1}_{!}D_{X^{C_{p}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{>1}_{!}D_{X^{C_{p}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{C_{p}}\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{C_{p}}(X_{!}D_{\underline{X}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{C_{p}}(X^{>1}_{!}\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{X}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{C_{p}}(X^{>1}_{!}\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{X}}\otimes\infl^{e}_{C_{p}}\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{tC_{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{!}D_{\underline{X}})^{tC_{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{>1}_{!}\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{X}})^{tC_{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{>1}_{!}\varepsilon^{*}D_{\underline{X}}\otimes\mathbb{Z})^{tC_{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{hC_{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{!}D_{\underline{X}})^{hC_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\underline{{X}}}^{C_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{C_{p}}c_{\underline{{X}}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\underline{{X}}}^{tC_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\underline{{X}}}^{hC_{p}}}

The rightmost part is induced by the ring map
\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{Z}
.
The violet square is obtained from
Observation

5.2.5
applied to
f=\varepsilon\colon\underline{{X^{C_{p}}}}\simeq\underline{{X}}^{>1}\rightarrow\underline{{X}}
and
D_{\underline{{X}}}
, using the equivalence
\Phi^{C_{p}}D_{\underline{{X}}}\simeq D_{X^{C_{p}}}
from
Theorem

2.3.3
and
\varepsilon^{C_{p}}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{C_{p}}}
.

By definition, the blue route recovers the gluing class.
Following the upper route to the same object gives a class having the desired properties. Indeed, on
\pi_{0}
the upper route reads

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{X^{C_{p}}}\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{X^{C_{p}}}\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{X^{C_{p}}}\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{X^{C_{p}}}\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{X^{C_{p}}}\mathbb{Z}/p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
proj

where
proj
refers to the sum of the projection maps
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/p
. Here, all maps in sight preserve the individual summands of
\bigoplus_{X^{C_{p}}}\mathbb{Z}
: the only potentially nonobvious case is the vertical red map, which is dealt with in
Observation

5.2.5
. ∎

Proof of
Theorem

D
.

Recall that Condition (H) from
Condition

1.3
asks about surjectivity of the upper composite in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\Gamma}_{d}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\displaystyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle E_{\Gamma}\mathrm{Fin}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip
